| server trailers conn-vs-stream scope | `cc2d9b873c` | `detect_error_scope.sh` (Angle D — READER_FNS extended) | `H2ServerConn.on_trailers` rejected a trailer section lacking END_STREAM with a bare `return error`, which bubbles through `serve()` → `send_goaway` and tears down the WHOLE connection, when every other malformed-request path in the same PR correctly uses `send_rst_stream(.protocol_error)` + `delete` (stream error, RFC 9113 §8.1.1). One bad request stream killed all streams on the connection. Codex P2 on #27569. **Why local review missed it:** `detect_error_scope.sh` targets exactly this class, but its `READER_FNS` list predated the server trailer/continuation/finalize handlers — `on_trailers`/`finalize_trailers`/`finalize_headers`/`on_continuation` were NOT in the list, so the detector never flagged line 437. AND the detector wasn't even run (abbreviated review). Fix (code): RST the stream; also decode the trailer block BEFORE the well-formedness check so a rejected trailer doesn't desync HPACK for later streams. Fix (process): added the server handlers to `READER_FNS`. **Repro:** `bash detect_error_scope.sh <git show cc2d9b873c:…/h2_server.v>` → flags `on_trailers` line 437; gone on `51c8732de8`. Commit `51c8732de8`. |

| server content-length validated only on last occurrence | `cc2d9b873c` | Angle A guard-completeness | `H2ServerConn.build_request` looped over header fields doing `content_length = f.value.int()` for each `content-length` field — the last occurrence overwrote earlier ones, so `content-length: 5` then `content-length: 0` with an empty body was accepted (the `0` matched the body), and both conflicting fields were still added to `req.header` for handlers/proxies to observe. RFC 9110 §8.6: differing duplicate content-lengths are malformed. Codex P2 on #27569. **Why local review missed it:** Angle A ("what does each branch do?") on a loop that *assigns* a singleton-field value must ask "what if it appears twice?" — the abbreviated review didn't. Class: **a repeatable-on-the-wire field that must be singleton, extracted with `x = f.value` inside a loop without a conflict check.** Fix: reject a second content-length whose value differs from the first. Commit `51c8732de8`. |

| HPACK decode failure handled as stream error (RST) not conn error | `84bdd8d2b4` | `detect_hpack_decode_scope.sh` (Angle E — RFC §4.3) | `H2ServerConn.finalize_headers`/`finalize_trailers` answered a `c.decoder.decode(...)` failure with `send_rst_stream(s.id, .compression_error)` (per-stream). But `H2HpackDecoder.decode` mutates the dynamic table AS it processes the block, so a failure leaves the decoder out of sync with the peer — every later header block on the connection decodes to garbage. RFC 9113 §4.3: a decode error MUST be a connection error of type COMPRESSION_ERROR (GOAWAY + close). The RST kept a corrupted connection alive. Codex P2 on #27569 (discussion_r3485226568). **This is the INVERSE of `detect_error_scope.sh`** (which flags conn-errors that should be per-stream); the existing detector can't catch a per-stream handler for a fault that must be connection-level, so a new detector was needed. Note the `finalize_headers` RST predated the PR (a latent gap the h2spec baseline already recorded as the PR3 hpack cluster); my PR replicated the same pattern in the new `finalize_trailers`. Fix: `send_goaway(.compression_error)` + `return` at both decode sites. **Bonus:** closed the whole h2spec HPACK error-scope cluster — 9 cases (hpack/2.3.3 ×2, 4.2, 5.2 ×3, 6.1, 6.3, http2/4.3), 120→129 pass. **Repro:** `detect_hpack_decode_scope.sh` flags lines 436/473 on `84bdd8d2b4`, clean on `d6cd7f8118`. **Lesson:** error-scope review is bidirectional — for every wire-fault class, ask BOTH "is this conn-error really per-stream?" (detect_error_scope) AND "is this per-stream RST really a connection error?" (HPACK decode, and any fault that desyncs connection-global state). Commit `d6cd7f8118`. |

| decode-then-discard for a RST'd-then-deleted stream | `9786c0ac32` | (no detector yet — see Angle D bullet) | `H2ServerConn.on_data`'s locally_reset fix drained in-flight DATA after the server sent RST_STREAM, but a further HEADERS/CONTINUATION for that SAME (now-deleted) stream id still hit `on_headers`'s "invalid client stream id" hard error — a connection-wide GOAWAY — because the fix only covered the `on_data` branch and never asked "what if the peer's in-flight frame is HEADERS, not DATA?" The stream struct itself is gone from `c.streams` (deleted at RST time to keep the concurrency-limit accounting correct), so there's no live `H2ServerStream.hpack_block` to accumulate into; the fix needed a connection-level scratch buffer (`discard_block`) plus a locally_reset-aware branch in BOTH `on_headers` and `on_continuation`. Codex P2 on #27589 (discussion_r3493008528). Local review (8-angle, medium effort) had run on this EXACT commit before push and returned `[]` — the finder angles asked "does DATA still work?" but never asked "does every OTHER frame type that can carry a HEADERS fragment (HEADERS, CONTINUATION) get the same treatment as DATA?" Class: **a fix that adds new state for ONE frame type must be checked against every OTHER frame type that can reach the same stream id.** |
| stale trailer_block redecoded on a second invalid HEADERS block | `9786c0ac32` | (no detector yet — see Angle D bullet) | `on_headers`'s existing-stream branch appended a further (invalid, post-response) HEADERS fragment onto `existing.trailer_block` WITHOUT clearing it first. If the stream had already completed a legitimate trailer section earlier (trailer_block populated, decoded once, but the stream lingered in `c.streams` because its response was still stalled on flow control), the stale already-decoded bytes were decoded a SECOND time along with the new fragment — reapplying the same HPACK incremental-indexing insertions twice and desyncing the shared dynamic table for every later stream on the connection. Same root cause as `detect_hpack_skip_on_half_closed.sh`'s lesson ("decode must run for every branch") but the INVERSE failure mode: decoding ran, but on a buffer that mixed data from two logically separate blocks. Codex P2 on #27589 (discussion_r3493616433). Class: **a per-stream scratch buffer that is appended-to across multiple, logically-separate HEADERS sequences (initial request, trailers, a stray extra block) must be cleared at the START of each new sequence, not just accumulated within one.** |
| unsound map-pruning by stream-id ordering | `9786c0ac32` | (no detector yet — see Angle D bullet) | The `locally_reset` tracking map (added to fix the DATA-after-RST case above) was pruned in `on_headers` with `for id in c.locally_reset.keys() { if id < frame.stream_id { delete } }`, reasoning "TCP ordering guarantees all in-flight frames for lower streams have already arrived." HTTP/2 only guarantees stream IDs are ALLOCATED in increasing order — it does NOT guarantee a client transmits all of stream N's frames before opening stream N+2; a client can legally interleave HEADERS(3) ahead of a queued DATA(1) in its own send buffer. Pruning on that false assumption deleted the tracking entry before the genuinely in-flight DATA(1) arrived, reintroducing the EXACT spurious-double-RST bug the map was built to fix. Codex P3 on #27589 (discussion_r3493616438). Class: **HTTP/2 stream-id monotonicity is a constraint on the SENDER's id-allocation order, not a guarantee about relative frame ARRIVAL order across streams — never use "id < N" as a proxy for "already delivered."** |
| GOAWAY last_stream_id counted refused/not-yet-processed streams | `fbbfe2c5a1` | (no detector yet — see Angle D bullet) | `send_goaway` reported `c.last_stream_id`, bumped in `on_headers` for ANY syntactically-valid stream id regardless of outcome — so a stream refused purely for exceeding the concurrency limit (RFC 9113 §5.1.2: REFUSED_STREAM means refused "prior to any processing", already told to the client via its own RST_STREAM as safe-to-retry) inflated GOAWAY's last_stream_id past streams whose outcome was genuinely uncertain. Not Codex-caught — flagged directly by the human reviewer; recorded here because the FIRST fix attempt (bump a new `last_processed_stream_id` inside `finalize_headers`) was itself caught as under-reporting by the SAME local review pass moments later: bumping only at `finalize_headers` time misses a stream still mid-CONTINUATION (HEADERS not yet fully assembled) when an UNRELATED error forces a GOAWAY before `finalize_headers` ever runs for it. Fix: bump at stream-CREATION time in `on_headers` (the same moment refusal is decided), not at finalize time. Class: **when adding an exclusion-based tracking field, bump it at the EARLIEST point the exclusion is decided, not at the point where the excluded/included work happens to run — a later-arriving connection-level error can fire before that point is ever reached.** |

| HPACK skip on half-closed HEADERS | `88a5d3435a` | `detect_hpack_skip_on_half_closed.sh` (Angle E) | `on_headers` RST-ed a post-END_STREAM HEADERS frame before decoding its HPACK block (`frame.fragment`). HPACK is connection-wide (RFC 7541 §2.2); skipping a block desyncs the dynamic table — a subsequent request referencing an entry from that block triggers GOAWAY(COMPRESSION_ERROR). The same PR had the comment "the decoder is stateful — skipping it would desync the dynamic table" for the REFUSED_STREAM path (§5.1.2) and the HPACK-error scope fix (#27569), but missed applying the same reasoning to the half-closed guard in the SAME function. Codex P2 on #27589 (review_4588368602, comment 3488814643). Fix: set `s.over_end = true` and route through `on_trailers`→`finalize_trailers` so the decode always runs before the RST (identical pattern to the refused-stream path). **Secondary finding surfaced during the fix (code-review Angle B):** when the post-END_STREAM HEADERS has `END_HEADERS=false`, `on_trailers` sets `c.awaiting_cont`; a legal client `RST_STREAM` on that stream then hits the `awaiting_cont != 0` guard in `dispatch_frame` and triggers GOAWAY instead of being processed as a stream cancel (RFC 9113 §6.4). Fixed by special-casing `H2RstStreamFrame` for the awaiting stream in the guard before the generic "expected CONTINUATION" error. **Lesson: when a correctness rule ("always decode before acting") applies to an event, enumerate ALL handler branches — Angle E caught the §5.1.2 branch and the HPACK-error branch but missed the half-closed branch three lines away. Secondary lesson: when a fix routes a new path through code that sets `c.awaiting_cont`, audit the `awaiting_cont != 0` guard in `dispatch_frame` for any legitimate frame the guard now incorrectly rejects.** Detector: `detect_hpack_skip_on_half_closed.sh` — exit 1 on `88a5d3435a` (no `over_end`), exit 0 after fix. Commit `9fe5720b1c`. |

| orphan CONTINUATION accepted on a locally-reset stream | `a61b12849c` | `on_continuation`'s discard branch checked `frame.stream_id in c.locally_reset` alone, with no check that a discard block was actually mid-assembly (`c.awaiting_cont == frame.stream_id`). A standalone CONTINUATION arriving on a stream id that was reset with a COMPLETE HEADERS block (so `awaiting_cont` never became that id) was silently decoded and discarded instead of being rejected as a §6.10 connection error — keeping an illegal frame sequence alive and feeding unvalidated bytes into the stateful HPACK decoder. Codex P2 on #27589 (pullrequestreview-4613845079). **Same miss class as the trailing-`awaiting_cont` bugs above, but Angle A/D never generalized it**: the existing dispatch_frame guard (`if c.awaiting_cont != 0 { ... }`) only validated CONTINUATION frames while a sequence WAS in progress; it never rejected one when `awaiting_cont == 0`, so any per-branch fix inside `on_continuation` (checking `locally_reset` alone) was structurally unable to close the gap — the invariant belongs at the single dispatch-level gate, not scattered per branch. Fix: extend the SAME `dispatch_frame` guard with an `else if frame is H2ContinuationFrame { return error(...) }` arm, so on_continuation is only ever entered with `awaiting_cont == frame.stream_id`. A human reviewer (not Codex) additionally caught that my FIRST fix attempt (a local `&& c.awaiting_cont == frame.stream_id` check inside on_continuation's locally_reset branch) only closed the hole for the reset-stream branch, leaving the identical gap open for a live (non-reset) stream that had already completed its HEADERS — the centralized-gate fix closes both. |
| unbounded `locally_reset` tracking map (DoS) | `a61b12849c` | `c.locally_reset[id] = true` (8 call sites) had no eviction — a peer that kept the connection open while repeatedly sending malformed/refused requests grew the map by one entry per stream id forever, even though `c.streams` itself is bounded to `h2_server_max_concurrent_streams`. Codex P2 on #27589 (pullrequestreview-4613845079); `detect_unbounded_accum.sh` would have flagged this if run (it flags `locally_reset_order: NO h2_max CAP` post-fix too — a **false positive** worth noting: the cap constant is named `h2_server_max_locally_reset_tracked`, not `h2_max_*`, so the detector's naming-convention grep misses it; confirmed by hand that `mark_locally_reset`'s eviction loop enforces the bound). Fix: `mark_locally_reset()` helper replacing all 8 direct-write sites, backed by a `locally_reset_order []u32` FIFO capped at `h2_server_max_locally_reset_tracked` (128), with eviction skipping the entry currently mid-discard (`== c.awaiting_cont`) so an in-progress drain is never evicted out from under itself. |
| classify_stream ignored locally_reset ids never opened via HEADERS | `a8152cd853` | `classify_stream` tested only `stream_id & 1 == 1 && stream_id <= last_stream_id` for closed. RFC 9113 §5.1 defines closed as entered when either side sends RST_STREAM, and explicitly permits remembering a reset stream in that state for a bounded window — exactly what `locally_reset` already tracks. A self-dependent PRIORITY frame (RFC 7540 §5.3.1, added in #27627) is legal on a stream the client never opened via HEADERS, so it can be `locally_reset` without ever advancing `last_stream_id`. Every caller of `classify_stream` (on_data, handle_control_frame's WINDOW_UPDATE arm, dispatch_frame's RST_STREAM arm) then misclassified later in-flight frames for that id as IDLE, forcing a spurious connection GOAWAY instead of draining them per §6.4. Codex P2 on #27627 (pullrequestreview-4618166175). A pasted external analysis suggested fixing this with three separate `frame.stream_id in c.locally_reset` checks inside each of the three callers instead; rejected in favor of the single-gate fix in `classify_stream` itself — same root-cause class as the orphan-CONTINUATION miss above (a per-branch check leaves a future fourth caller uncovered), and RFC 9113 §5.1's own text supports treating a locally-reset id as genuinely `closed`, not a separate implementation-only concept layered outside the state model. |
| self-dependent-PRIORITY guard re-RST'd a normally-closed stream | `9d29f2786d` | The RFC 7540 §5.3.1 self-dependency guard exempted only `frame.stream_id in c.locally_reset` from re-sending RST_STREAM. A stream that completed NORMALLY (`run_request` deletes it from `c.streams` without ever touching `locally_reset`) was misclassified as still eligible for a fresh RST, sending a non-PRIORITY frame on an already-closed stream (RFC 9113 §5.1) — exactly the violation the exemption existed to avoid, just for the wrong subset of "closed." Codex P2 on #27627 (pullrequestreview-4620220448); same root file as the row above, found in the very NEXT review round after that fix landed — the earlier fix generalized classify_stream but this sibling guard (`H2PriorityFrame` in `dispatch_frame`) still used the narrower pre-generalization check. Fix: gate on `classify_stream(...) != .closed` (reusing the same classify_stream generalization) instead of the locally_reset-only check — idle/active still get RST (self-dependency is unconditionally a stream error per §5.3.1), any closed stream is left alone regardless of HOW it closed. A pasted external analysis proposed `== .idle` instead, subtly wrong: it would silently tolerate a self-dependent PRIORITY on a currently ACTIVE stream (e.g. one whose response is stalled mid-flow-control, still in `c.streams`), a real protocol violation §5.3.1 says MUST be a stream error regardless of open/idle state — only CLOSED exempts. **Lesson: when a fix generalizes ONE consumer of a helper (classify_stream) to close a gap, grep for OTHER consumers of the SAME helper's narrower predecessor (`locally_reset` direct checks) that were not migrated — the fix landed in a prior commit but a sibling call site three lines away in the SAME function kept using the old, narrower test.** |
| refused-stream check masked self-dependency error in finalize_headers | (pending, before next commit) | `finalize_headers` checked `s.refused` (over the concurrency limit, RFC 9113 §5.1.2) BEFORE `s.self_dep` (RFC 7540 §5.3.1 priority self-dependency), so a HEADERS frame that was BOTH over-limit AND self-dependent answered `RST_STREAM(REFUSED_STREAM)` instead of `PROTOCOL_ERROR`. REFUSED_STREAM specifically tells the client the request is safe to retry unchanged elsewhere — but a self-dependent request is malformed independent of the concurrency limit, so retrying it verbatim would just repeat the same error. Codex P2 on #27627 (pullrequestreview-4620412384); found in the very NEXT review round after the sibling classify_stream/self-dep fixes above, in the SAME `finalize_headers` function those changes were adjacent to but didn't touch. Fix: swap the check order so `self_dep` is evaluated first (both branches still route through the identical decode-then-RST pattern, so no HPACK-sync regression). **Lesson: when two independent boolean flags on the same struct (`refused`, `self_dep`) are both checked with early-return branches in the same function, the CHECK ORDER encodes an implicit priority between error classes — audit that order whenever a new flag is added alongside an existing one, since "decoded fine, add a second gate" reads safe but can silently let an earlier gate's return value mask the new one's more-specific diagnosis.** |
| **Process miss (doc-sync needs review too):** a same-session doc-sync edit (README.md/run_h2spec.sh updated for the empty h2spec baseline) itself introduced 3 fresh issues — a 193-char markdown line (repo's 100-char limit), a wrong PR count ("Four" vs 3 listed numbers), and stale prose left contradicting the new empty-baseline statement two bullets below it. Codex P3 x3 on #27627 (pullrequestreview-4619906229). None were code-review misses in the A–G sense (no `.v` file involved) — they were plain proofreading gaps in prose written to satisfy the commit-gate's doc-sync rule. Lesson: a doc-only commit still needs a pass for internal consistency (does this paragraph still agree with the one below it after the edit?) and a line-length check (`awk 'length>100'` or `./vnew check-md`), not just "the fact stated is now correct." |
| **Process miss, not a code miss:** the local review that should have caught both rows above was run informally (ad hoc reasoning against the diff) instead of executing the actual mandated `/code-review` protocol — zero mechanical detectors were invoked, the diff was scoped to `git diff HEAD` instead of the PR-scope diff against `master`, and no Phase 1 angle output was produced. The user caught this directly ("You failed to run our custom code review"). Re-running properly (detectors via WSL, full Phase 0–2) surfaced no NEW bugs beyond what Codex had already found — but that is luck, not process; an eyeballed review is not a review. Lesson: reasoning fluently about a diff is not a substitute for invoking the tool. |
| socket-poll cannot see TLS-buffered app data | `6bf4a5934c` | `detect_tls_read_precheck_poll.sh` (new Angle F bullet) | `H2PooledTransport.read()` (vlib/net/http/h2_pooled_transport.v) called a raw-socket `wait_for_read()` BEFORE ever calling the wrapped `SSLConn.read()`. mbedtls_ssl_read()/SSL_read() return at most the caller's buffer size per call; if one underlying socket read pulled in several TLS records at once (routine once a response exceeds `h2_conn_read_chunk`=16KB), the remainder stays decrypted-and-buffered inside the TLS library with NO new socket bytes needed to retrieve it. A raw select()-based poll cannot see that buffered data — it only sees the SOCKET's own pending bytes — so it times out even though `conn.read()` would return immediately; `H2MuxConn.read_loop` treats that timeout as a benign wake-up and just retries, so the buffered response is never drained until unrelated new socket data arrives, or never. Codex P1 on vlang/v#27643 (pullrequestreview-4626225521, discussion 3520259789). **Why local review missed it:** this is a NEW bug class this repo's angles had no coverage for — TLS-adapter design (Angle F's "Transport / external-object thread-safety" covers *concurrent* read/write safety, not a socket-poll's blindness to library-internal buffering). Fix: delete the outer poll; `conn.read()` always tries a non-blocking-in-effect decode of buffered data first, falling back to its own internal, now-shortened (200ms→50ms) bounded WANT_READ wait only when genuinely nothing is buffered — the shrink keeps a writer's wait for `io_mu` bounded now that the reader holds it for the whole locked call instead of only when data is already known-ready. **Repro:** `detect_tls_read_precheck_poll.sh vlib/net/http/h2_pooled_transport.v 6bf4a5934c` → flags `read()` line 98 (exit 1); clean on the fixed tree (exit 0). Class: **a socket-level readiness poll is not equivalent to "does the wrapped library have data to return" for any transport with its own internal buffering (TLS, or any other stream library that can hold more than it hands back per call) — such adapters must let the wrapped call drive its own bounded wait instead of gating entry with an outer poll.** |
| close_idle() released a pooled h2 connection's pool reference twice | `6bf4a5934c` | runtime test `test_h2_pool_close_idle_release_is_idempotent` (transport_h2_test.v) | `Transport.close_idle()` collected every `h2_conns` entry under `t.mu` and released each outside the lock, but never removed the entry from the map — only the connection's own `close_transport` closure did that, and only once its refcount reached zero. A connection with a genuinely in-flight stream survives ONE release (refcount drops but doesn't hit zero), so it stayed registered; a second `close_idle()` call (concurrent caller, or simply calling it twice) found the SAME connection again and released it a second time, over-decrementing the refcount below the number of live references — confirmed directly (`refs=0` while `active_streams=1`) rather than inferred through the in-flight fetch's eventual status, since that outcome depends on unrelated timing between the teardown and the reader thread noticing it. Codex P2 on vlang/v#27643 (pullrequestreview-4626225521, discussion 3520259791). Fix: clear `h2_conns`/`h2_dial_id` in the SAME locked critical section that collects the entries to release, so no caller (repeat or concurrent) can ever observe — and therefore re-release — a connection this call has already claimed; the connection's own `close_transport` closure's dial_id guard already no-ops safely when it later finds its map entry gone. **Repro:** the new test fails with `refs=0`/`active_streams=1` on the buggy code, passes after clearing the maps. Class: **a "collect under lock, release outside lock" refcounted-pool sweep must remove what it collects from the shared map atomically with the collection — otherwise a connection surviving one release (refcount didn't reach zero) is re-collectible by a repeat sweep, over-releasing it.** |
| dial-failure error code lost across the singleflight boundary | `339d7ebf19` | runtime test `test_h2_pool_dial_failure_preserves_error_code` (new Angle A bullet) | `h2_dial_failed` took the caught error's `.msg()` string only and reconstructed it via plain `error(msg)`, throwing away `.code()`. `request.v`'s outer retry loop (`is_no_need_retry_error`) decides whether to give up early on specific codes (`net.err_connect_timed_out`, `net.err_timed_out_code`, …) — collapsing a coded dial failure to an uncoded one made a fundamentally hopeless failure retry up to `max_retries` times instead of failing fast, a regression from the pre-pooling one-shot h2 path (which propagated the raw `IError`, code intact). Codex P2 on vlang/v#27643 (pullrequestreview-4627654418, discussion 3521494715). **Why local review missed it:** no existing angle specifically asks "does converting an error to a string and reconstructing it via `error(...)` drop its code?" — Angle A's "error swallowed in `or {}`" bullet is about DROPPED errors, not errors that survive but lose their code identity. Fix: thread the caught `IError` itself (not `.msg()`) through `h2_dial_failed`/`H2DialCall.err_code`, reconstructing with `error_with_code(msg, code)` when `code != 0`. **Repro:** dial a closed local port; compare the pooled path's `err.code()` against a raw direct dial's own code for the identical target — got `0` vs `-68` on the buggy code, matched after the fix. Class: **any time an error is carried across a thread/struct-field boundary (a singleflight result, a deferred callback, a stored "last error" field) by extracting `.msg()` into a `string` field, its `.code()` is silently discarded unless a parallel `int` field carries it too — grep for a `string`-typed error field with no sibling `code`/`_code int` field.** |
| new h2 connection pool didn't inherit Transport.max_idle_conns | `339d7ebf19` | runtime test `test_h2_pool_respects_max_idle_conns_cap` (new Angle F-adjacent bullet) | `h2_conns` (the pooled h2-connection map added in the same PR) had no eviction of its own — a client touching many distinct h2 origins accumulated one live TLS socket + reader thread per origin forever, ignoring `max_idle_conns` (a limit the h1 pool, in the very same `Transport`, already enforces via `checkin`'s `evict_oldest_locked`). This was an EXPLICITLY documented "known follow-up, out of scope" in the original PR body rather than a silent oversight, but Codex re-flagged it as P2 on the next round anyway (vlang/v#27643 pullrequestreview-4627654418, discussion 3521494717) and the user asked for a real fix, not a deferral. Fix: `evict_idle_h2_if_over_cap` (transport.v) — the h2 pool's analog of `checkin`'s h1 eviction, called from the SAME locked registration section as the new entry's insertion, evicting only a currently-IDLE (`active_streams==0`) h2 connection, least-recently-idle first, never a busy one. **Repro:** 3 sequential dials to 3 distinct h2-only origins with `max_idle_conns=1` left `h2_conns.len==3` on the buggy code, `<=1` after the fix. Class: **when a PR adds a second pool/cache structure alongside an existing one on the same parent object (`Transport`), audit every one of the parent's PUBLIC capacity/lifetime knobs (`max_idle_conns`, `max_idle_conns_per_host`, `idle_timeout`) against the new structure individually — "it has its own separate map" does not exempt it from limits the object's own documented contract already promises callers.** |
| ALPN-probe waiters didn't reuse the h1 pool the dialer just populated | `339d7ebf19` | runtime test `test_h2_pool_waiter_reuses_h1_pool_after_alpn_probe` | `h2_await_dial` (waiters behind the singleflight ALPN probe) unconditionally called `tls_fresh_round_trip` — its OWN fresh dial — when the probe resolved to http/1.1, even though the dialer (in the SAME PR, fixed in the prior Codex round) already completes its own probe connection and checks it into `h1_idle`. Codex P3 on vlang/v#27643 (pullrequestreview-4627654418, discussion 3521494719). **Why local review missed it:** this is the SAME "sibling handler scan (adjacent-arm miss)" class already in this corpus (classify_stream/self-dep rows below) — an EXISTING angle, not a coverage gap: the dialer's h1-fallback branch and the waiter's h1-fallback branch are two "arms" of the identical decision (`ssl_conn.negotiated_alpn() != 'h2'`), and fixing one without re-checking the other repeated the exact miss pattern this repo has hit at least three times before (classify_stream, self-dep guard, refused-vs-self_dep order). Fix: `h2_await_dial` tries `t.checkout(key)` before falling back to a fresh dial; this only works because the FIX also had to move `call.cv.broadcast()` in the dialer to AFTER its own `maybe_checkin` (previously the broadcast fired before the exchange even started, so waiters always raced ahead of the checkin and found nothing to reuse regardless). **Process note:** the checkout-first waiter logic was applied in the same edit as the error-code fix above WITHOUT a prior failing test — caught mid-session by re-reading the Phase R ordering rule, reverted, reproduced properly (4/4 accepts on buggy code, 3/3 runs), then reapplied. Lesson: batching an unrelated second fix into the same edit as a properly-sequenced one is how Phase R silently gets skipped for the second fix. |
| read-timeout watchdog thread lingers for the full deadline after its request completes | (self-caught during `/vreview` on the same commit, before push) | n/a — caught reading the new code, not via an external reviewer | The first version of `h2_stream_read_timeout_watchdog` (added to fix the read_timeout-enforcement finding above) did one bare `time.sleep(timeout)` then checked the stream. Since `req.read_timeout` defaults to 30s and EVERY pooled h2 request now spawns one of these, a request completing in milliseconds still leaves its watchdog thread sleeping for up to 30s before it wakes, finds the stream already done, and exits — under sustained throughput on a busy connection this accumulates one lingering thread per recent request. Fix: poll in bounded ~200ms steps, checking `s.ended` after each, exiting as soon as the request completes instead of always waiting out the full deadline. Class: **a per-request watchdog/timer goroutine whose lifetime is bounded only by the CONFIGURED deadline (not by the request's actual, usually much shorter, completion time) turns "one thread per request" into "one thread per request lingering for the full timeout" — bound its poll granularity independently of the deadline itself.** |

**Phase R note (#27569, three rows):** these were caught by Codex on a PR where the
local "review" was a one-paragraph self-summary — the 8 angles and the mechanical
detectors were NOT actually run. Running `detect_error_scope.sh` (after the
READER_FNS fix) flags the trailers bug; a genuine Angle-A pass flags the
content-length loop. **Bonus:** running the FULL process afterward (requirements-
driven Angle E, RFC 9113 §8.2.1) found a THIRD issue NEITHER Codex nor h2spec
caught — request field *values* weren't checked for NUL/CR/LF (a header-injection
/ smuggling vector if proxied to h1), fixed in `84bdd8d2b4`. The full process
pays for itself; the abbreviated version is the miss generator.

**The through-line:** every miss was a cross-cutting consistency/conformance
property, and most were *self-inflicted* — an angle existed but was eyeballed
instead of run. Mechanical checks (grep/script that exits non-zero) are the fix;
prose angles get skipped. The reproduction step also caught a too-shallow fix:
content-length was guarded upstream but the parse site (wait_response u64()) still
read UNGUARDED until a local guard was added.

| self-referential C connection struct copied by value | `9017d174dd` | `detect_pooled_conn_stored_by_value.sh` (new Angle F/G bullet) | `H2PooledTransport.conn` stored the dialed `ssl.SSLConn` as a bare value field. `SSLConn.dial()` wires `mbedtls_ssl_set_bio(&s.ssl, &s.server_fd, ...)` — a raw C pointer into the STRUCT'S OWN ADDRESS at dial time. `H2PooledTransport{conn: conn}` bitwise-copied that struct into a new memory location, so the copy's internal `p_bio` still pointed at the ORIGINAL dialed object's `server_fd`, not the copy's own — every later read/write/close operated on stale/orphaned state instead of the live connection. Codex P1 on vlang/v#27643 (pullrequestreview-4631763931, discussion 3525390891). **Why local review missed it:** no existing angle asks "does this C-interop wrapper type have internal self-pointers, and if so, is it ever assigned into a value-typed struct field?" — Angle G's `unsafe{}` scrutiny bullet covers raw pointer arithmetic inside `unsafe` blocks, not a plain value-copy of an opaque struct that HAPPENS to embed C self-pointers set up by an earlier call. The correct idiom was already established one file over (`H1PooledConn.ssl` in transport.v is `&ssl.SSLConn`), so this was also a sibling-search miss — a fresh pooled-transport type was written without checking how the existing pooled type stores the identical kind of connection. Fix: `conn &ssl.SSLConn`; constructor param `mut conn &ssl.SSLConn` (a bare `&ssl.SSLConn` without `mut` cannot call mut-receiver methods — confirmed by the V compiler, not assumed). **Repro:** `detect_pooled_conn_stored_by_value.sh vlib/net/http/h2_pooled_transport.v 9017d174dd` flags line 87 (exit 1); clean on the fixed tree (exit 0). Class: **any struct wrapping a C-interop connection type whose setup call (`dial`/`connect`/`accept`) wires internal pointers to the struct's OWN address must be stored by pointer everywhere it's held for later reuse — a value copy silently detaches the copy's C state from the original object's fields.** Commit `22921ba7af`. |
| singleflight wake delayed until after unshareable work, head-of-line-blocking waiters | `9017d174dd` | (no detector — design/timing judgment call, see Angle D/F bullet) | `h2_dial_and_do`'s ALPN-fallback-to-h1 branch didn't wake singleflight waiters (`call.cv.broadcast()`) until AFTER its own `conn.exchange()` fully completed and checked the connection in — a deliberate round-3 design choice so a waiter had a real chance to reuse the just-dialed h1 connection. But HTTP/1.1 cannot share one connection across two concurrent requests, so every waiter queued behind however long the DIALER's own response took — unbounded head-of-line blocking for a slow/streaming response, a regression from ordinary (non-probing) h1 pooling where concurrent first-time callers to a fresh origin dial independently. Codex P2 on vlang/v#27643 (pullrequestreview-4631763931, discussion 3525390892). **Why local review missed it:** this is a genuinely NEW angle — existing coverage (Angle F's "condition-variable liveness / wake invariant") only asks "does every state-mutation call broadcast," never "is the broadcast timed as EARLY as the waiters' own contract allows." A singleflight coordinator that does `useful_work(); wake()` instead of `wake(); useful_work()` passes every existing liveness check (it DOES eventually wake) while still serializing unrelated callers behind the useful work's duration. User confronted the resulting trade-off directly (this reverses a round-3 test's exact invariant) and settled it against RFC 9112 §9.4 (multiple connections exist specifically to avoid this blocking) rather than a code-only judgment call. Fix: wake `call` immediately once the fact waiters actually need (protocol resolved) is known, before running any of this caller's own unshareable work. **No mechanical detector**: "is this the earliest point a wake COULD happen" requires understanding what data a waiter's predicate actually depends on, which is a semantic question a grep cannot answer generically — left as a prose bullet with a worked example. Class: **for any singleflight/coordinator pattern, ask separately "does every path eventually wake waiters" (mechanizable, already covered) and "is the wake positioned before or after the SPECIFIC piece of work that isn't relevant to what waiters need to know" (not mechanizable — trace what the waiter's own branch after waking actually depends on).** Commit `22921ba7af`. |
| singleflight registration TOCTOU across a released-then-reacquired lock | `9017d174dd` | (no detector — see Angle F bullet; too context-specific to generalize safely) | `h2_round_trip` checked `t.h2_conns[key]` under `t.mu`, released the lock, then called `h2_dial_and_do`, which only checked `t.dialing[key]` before committing to a brand-new singleflight dial. A concurrent dial that finished in the window between those two lock acquisitions was invisible to `h2_dial_and_do`'s ONE check, causing a redundant TLS/ALPN handshake and orphaning the connection that had just won. Codex P2 on vlang/v#27643 (pullrequestreview-4631763931, discussion 3525390895). **Why local review missed it:** the checker (`h2_round_trip`) and the committer (`h2_dial_and_do`) are two DIFFERENT functions, so a per-function TOCTOU scan (Angle F's existing lock-scope bullets all look for a gap WITHIN one function between reading a value and using it) never considered the gap BETWEEN a caller's own check and a callee's separate, later check of the SAME shared state. Fix: `h2_dial_and_do` rechecks `t.h2_conns[key]`/`t.key_proto[key]` under a fresh lock at its own entry, before creating a new singleflight coordinator — closing the caller/callee gap down to the (much smaller, already-accepted-elsewhere) gap between that recheck and the SECOND `t.dialing[key]` check right after it. **Impact-analysis catch during the same fix:** the new fast path lacked the `h2_err_retryable_code` fallthrough retry the SIBLING fast path (`h2_round_trip`'s own) already has for the identical race (a connection that looks usable but was GOAWAY'd/closed the instant before registration) — added to match. Class: **a "check under lock, release, act" pattern is not just a within-function risk — when the CHECK and the COMMITTING ACTION live in two different functions (a fast-path caller and a slow-path callee it falls through to), the callee must redo the SAME check under its own fresh lock acquisition before committing to expensive/exclusive work, not trust that the caller's now-stale check is still true.** Commit `22921ba7af`. |
| caller-callback execution time counted against a network-wait timeout | `9017d174dd` | (no detector — see note on why an end-to-end repro is structurally impossible here) | The h2 pooled-client response watchdog stayed armed while `wait_response()` ran the caller's `on_data`/progress callback OUTSIDE the stream lock (deliberately, so a slow callback never stalls the reader thread's delivery to OTHER streams) — but the watchdog's `since()` read `s.last_activity`, a field the callback's own duration never touched, so a callback slower than `read_timeout` got its stream falsely reset even though nothing was actually waiting on the network. Codex P2 on vlang/v#27643 (pullrequestreview-4631763931, discussion 3525390898). **Why local review missed it:** no existing angle asks "for a timeout documented as bounding NETWORK wait time, does ANY non-network activity (a caller callback, a downstream write, disk I/O) run during the window the timeout measures, and if so, is that activity's duration excluded from the clock?" Fix: `H2MuxStream.in_callback bool`, set around the callback call; `h2_response_phase_since` returns `time.now()` (clock paused) while true, so the watchdog's elapsed-time computation stays ~0 for the callback's whole duration however long it runs, and a genuine post-callback network stall is still caught normally once `last_activity` stops advancing. **No mechanical detector, and no end-to-end behavioral test either — verified this empirically, don't assume it**: the reader thread updates `s.ended`/`last_activity` completely independent of the callback's progress (same reason the callback runs unlocked), so ANY test where the peer delivers its frames promptly reaches `s.ended == true` well before the watchdog's poll-after-sleep `done()` check can ever observe an expired deadline — a full peer+watchdog round-trip test is a FALSE GUARD here regardless of which timing values are chosen, because the bug is only reachable when genuine flow-control backpressure blocks the peer from sending more data until the callback finishes and credits the window back. Used a direct unit test of the mechanism instead (asserts `h2_response_phase_since` returns a fresh timestamp instead of a deliberately-staled `last_activity` while `in_callback` is forced true) — it fails to even COMPILE on the pre-fix code (the field doesn't exist), which is itself a valid Phase-R signal for this class of defect. Class: **when a timeout is documented as bounding ONE specific kind of wait (network I/O), and the implementation deliberately allows OTHER kinds of work (a caller callback) to run concurrently/unlocked during that window for an unrelated, legitimate reason (not stalling other consumers), that other work's duration must be explicitly excluded from the timeout's clock — and because the exclusion mechanism is usually orthogonal to whatever makes the wait "network-shaped" in the first place, an end-to-end test through the normal happy path will not exercise it if anything else in the design (like this file's reader/consumer decoupling) races ahead of the callback and closes the window before the buggy behavior can fire.** Commit `22921ba7af`. |
| idle-cap check and eviction-scan eligibility mismatch within one pool | `9017d174dd` | `detect_idle_cap_counts_busy_conns.sh` (extends the pool-cap-scope-symmetry class, new angle dimension) | `evict_oldest_idle_locked`'s cap check summed `t.total_idle_locked() + t.h2_conns.len` — `h2_conns.len` counts EVERY h2 connection regardless of activity — but the eviction scan two dozen lines below only ever considers h2 entries with `active_streams == 0`. A busy h2 connection inflated the CHECK's total without ever being a candidate the SCAN could act on, so a pool with busy h2 connections and one genuinely idle h1 connection could look over budget purely from the busy count, and the unrelated idle h1 connection got wrongly evicted to "fix" a budget that was never actually exceeded. Codex P3 on vlang/v#27643 (pullrequestreview-4631763931, discussion 3525390899). **This is a DIFFERENT dimension of the same function's known asymmetry problem** — `detect_pool_cap_scope_symmetry.sh` (already in this corpus, round 4) catches a cap check that doesn't reference BOTH sibling pools (h1_idle vs h2_conns); this bug has both pools referenced correctly but doesn't reference the SAME ELIGIBILITY FILTER (busy vs idle) as the action that follows it, WITHIN one already-correctly-scoped pool. Fix: replace the bare `.len` with a loop counting only `active_streams == 0` entries, matching the scan's own filter exactly. **My own first fix attempt was itself wrong, caught only by running the full test suite (not by re-reading the fix against Codex's specific complaint):** excluding `just_registered_h2_key` from the COUNT too (mirroring how the scan excludes it from CANDIDACY) broke an unrelated existing test (`test_h2_pool_respects_max_idle_conns_cap`) — a just-registered h2 connection's `active_streams` is still 0 before `do_h2` runs, so excluding it from the count under-counted the pool during sequential single-connection dials to distinct origins, letting the combined total sit AT the cap instead of over it and silently skipping an eviction that should have happened. The just-registered key must stay excluded from CANDIDACY (it can never evict itself — that's the round-4 fix) but must be INCLUDED in the COUNT (it occupies a real pool slot the instant it's registered, whether or not it can be picked as a victim). **Repro:** `detect_idle_cap_counts_busy_conns.sh vlib/net/http/transport.v 9017d174dd` flags the bare `h2_conns.len` (exit 1); clean on the fixed tree (exit 0) — note this detector catches the ORIGINAL Codex-reported shape (bare unfiltered `.len`) but would NOT have caught my own follow-up mistake (a filtered count that additionally, incorrectly, excludes an entry the filter alone wouldn't exclude) — that class needs a semantic check this repo does not yet have a mechanical detector for. Class: **a cap CHECK and the eviction/candidacy ACTION that follows it in the same function must count using the IDENTICAL eligibility predicate — when the action's predicate has more than one clause (here: "active_streams==0" AND "not the just-registered key"), verify the check applies the SAME clauses, not just the same POOLS.** Commit `22921ba7af`. |
| idle-eviction race releases a pooled h2 connection's refcount twice | (self-caught during a `/vreview` re-run, zero code changes since round 5) | `detect_ungated_release_after_conditional_delete.sh` (new Angle F bullet) | `h2_round_trip`'s idle-expiry eviction branch guarded `t.h2_conns.delete(key)`/`t.h2_dial_id.delete(key)` with `if voidptr(existing) == voidptr(conn)` (added in round 5 so a caller that lost the removal race skips the delete), but the following `conn.shutdown_when_idle()`/`conn.release()` ran UNCONDITIONALLY. Two concurrent `round_trip` calls to the same origin racing the same idle-expired connection — or a concurrent `Transport.close_idle()`, which releases every h2 conn unconditionally by design — both fell through to the ungated `release()`, over-decrementing `H2MuxConn.refs` (verified empirically: `refs == -1` with two racers, via a temporary instrumented `__global` min-refs tracker, `-enable-globals` build flag). **Not a Codex finding** — found unprompted on a `/vreview` re-run with zero code changes since round 5, via the new "fresh eyes, no checklist" Phase 1 pass (rev 16 of `code-review-reference.html`): the branch that introduced the gap was itself round 5's own fix for a DIFFERENT finding (idle_timeout not enforced), and neither that round's own A-G `/vreview` pass nor Codex caught it. **Why local review missed it the first time:** `shutdown_when_idle()` sitting right next to `release()` looks equally safe to call twice — one genuinely is idempotent (just sets a bool), the other decrements a shared refcount — so a fix that guards the map delete reads as "obviously this whole block is now race-safe" without checking that EVERY statement in the block shares the same "exactly once" requirement. Fix: compute `won_removal := voidptr(existing) == voidptr(conn)` once, gate BOTH `shutdown_when_idle()` and `release()` on it, not just the map `.delete()` calls — mirroring `close_idle()`'s own "clear the map atomically with collection" pattern and `evict_oldest_idle_locked`'s atomic delete-and-return-victim pattern, just applied to the OTHER half of the branch. **Repro:** new permanent regression test `test_h2_pool_idle_evict_race_does_not_double_release` (transport_h2_test.v — reads `t.h2_conns[key]` directly via same-module private-field access to capture the connection pointer before the race, spawns two concurrent racers, asserts `pooled.refs == 0` after) fails with `refs == -1` on the pre-fix commit, passes after. Posted as a PR review comment (pullrequestreview-4636271901) BEFORE applying the fix, per explicit user instruction — a deliberate deviation from `/codex-respond`'s normal fix-then-reply order, appropriate here since there was no external reviewer round to fold the reply into. Class: **a TOCTOU guard added to protect ONE side effect in a branch (a shared-map delete) does not automatically protect every OTHER side effect in that same branch sharing the identical "exactly once, only by the winner" requirement (a refcount release) — an idempotent call sitting immediately next to a non-idempotent one in an ungated block is the tell; enumerate every statement in a race-guarded branch, not just the first one a fix happened to gate.** Commit `d8053872bf`. |

| platform-capability gate lost when mirroring a sibling's FUNCTION but not its CALL PATH | `e0368d7a2d` | `detect_vschannel_pooled_alpn_gate.sh` (new Angle C nuance: caller-scope sibling parity) | `h2_dial_probe_vschannel` (new in vlang/v#27712, Phase 4 vschannel h2 pooling) dialed via `new_vschannel_pooled_transport`, which unconditionally installs an ALPN wire buffer that the handshake injects as `SECBUFFER_APPLICATION_PROTOCOLS`. Pre-Windows 8.1 / Server 2012 R2 SChannel has no client-side ALPN and can fail the WHOLE handshake on that buffer — and the PR's `round_trip` narrowing put the pooled probe BEFORE the one-shot fallback, so on those systems ordinary HTTPS would fail outright instead of degrading to plain HTTP/1.1 (a regression from the pre-pooling behavior). Codex P1 on vlang/v#27712 (pullrequestreview-4729311285, discussion 3609206168). **Why local review missed it:** the sibling-parity check (Phase 0 step 7) RAN — the adapter's own doc comment says "Mirrors vschannel_h2_do's connect sequence" — but the parity diff was scoped to the sibling FUNCTION's body. The ALPN capability gate (`C.vschannel_alpn_supported() != 0`) lives one level ABOVE the sibling, in its caller `vschannel_ssl_do` — a precondition the sibling's call path establishes, invisible to a function-body diff. Fix: gate at the probe (the single funnel for pooled vschannel dials): `vschannel_alpn_supported() == 0` → return `is_h2: false` WITHOUT dialing; the caller memoizes `key_proto[key] = 1` so every later request short-circuits to the one-shot path, whose own guard then dials h1 with no ALPN — the exact pre-pooling endpoint. **Repro (static — runtime impossible on Win 11, `alpn_supported()` is hard-true and can't be faked without hooking ntdll):** `detect_vschannel_pooled_alpn_gate.sh vlib/net/http/vschannel_pooled_transport_windows.c.v e0368d7a2d` flags (exit 1); clean on the fixed tree (exit 0). Class: **when new code mirrors an existing sibling function, diff not just the sibling's BODY but every precondition its CALLERS establish on the path to it (capability gates, platform checks, admission guards) — a guard living in the sibling's caller is part of the sibling's contract, and a mirrored path that enters at a different altitude must reproduce it.** Commit `2ac1373390`. |
| hand-rolled C matcher weaker than the existing V parser it must agree with (substring vs token) | `a1b14edda6` | (no static detector — runtime regression test in-repo is stronger; deliberate call) | `http_response_complete` (vschannel.c, vlang/v#27714) decided chunk framing via `http_ci_contains(te, "chunked")` — a substring match — while the V layer that later decodes the same response (`parse_response`/`has_header_token`) matches only real comma-separated transfer-coding tokens. A response with `Transfer-Encoding: xchunked` whose raw body bytes accidentally look like a complete chunked message (e.g. starting `0\r\n\r\n`) made the C read loop stop early while V (correctly) left the body undecoded — a silently truncated body. Codex P2 on vlang/v#27714 (pullrequestreview-4689847198, discussion 3575137170). **Why local review missed it:** the sibling-parity check looks for siblings in the same file/module family — the authoritative sibling here was in a DIFFERENT LANGUAGE AND LAYER (V's `has_header_token`, whose token semantics the C helper must agree with because both act on the same wire bytes); nothing prompted a cross-layer semantic-agreement diff. Fix: `http_has_transfer_coding` mirrors `has_header_token` exactly (split on commas, strip `;params`, trim OWS, ci-compare whole tokens). **Repro (runtime):** `test_vschannel_h1_transfer_encoding_extension_token_is_not_chunked` — body truncated to the 5-byte fake terminator on pre-fix code, full raw body after. Class: **when two layers (a C fast-path and a V parser, a framing decision and a decode decision) each interpret the SAME wire field, they must share ONE semantics — locate the existing authoritative parser and mirror it exactly; any hand-rolled weaker approximation (substring, prefix, lenient parse) WILL disagree on some input, and the disagreement is the bug.** Commit `a58f706b36`. |
| conservative-fallback design silently reverts to pre-fix behavior on legal input variants (trailing OWS) | `a1b14edda6` | (no static detector — runtime regression test in-repo; deliberate call) | `http_find_header` (vschannel.c, vlang/v#27714) trimmed only LEADING spaces/tabs from header values, so a legal `Content-Length: 27 ` (RFC 7230 field-value grammar allows OWS on both sides) failed the deliberately-strict digit parse, and the design's safe fallback — "anything unparseable → read until close" — quietly reinstated the exact keep-alive hang the whole feature exists to fix, for any server emitting trailing whitespace. Codex P2 on vlang/v#27714 (pullrequestreview-4689847198, discussion 3575137175). **Why local review missed it:** the strict-parse-with-safe-fallback shape reads as "can't be wrong, worst case is the old behavior" — which frames spurious fallbacks as harmless when here the old behavior IS the bug; and testing used only canonical header formatting, never legal variants. Fix: trim trailing OWS in the shared extractor (both consumers benefit). **Repro (runtime):** `test_vschannel_h1_content_length_with_trailing_whitespace_still_frames` — waited out the peer's full 3s close on pre-fix code (3.054s measured), completes <1.5s after. Class: **when a strict parse gates a fix whose fallback is the pre-fix behavior, every SPURIOUS parse failure converts a legal input into a silent regression — normalize all grammar-legal variations (OWS, case, ordering) BEFORE the strict check, and Phase-R the fix against legal-variant inputs, not only canonical ones.** Commit `a58f706b36`. |

| behavior change verified only in its own module; consumer tests in sibling modules broke (one masked by the flaky list) | `2ac1373390` (found by CI, not review) | (no detector — mechanical sweep command in the narrative) | vlang/v#27712's `round_trip` narrowing makes an h2-enabled https request to an h1-only origin open TWO connections on native Windows (ALPN probe + one-shot dial). Verification ran `vlib/net/http/` (both configs) + smoke tests — but single-accept https test servers CONSUMING `http.fetch` live in OTHER modules: `vlib/net/mbedtls/mbedtls_head_with_content_length_test.v` failed outright on all 3 Windows CI jobs (probe consumed the one accept slot → deferred `listener.shutdown()` → real request ECONNREFUSED 10061), and `mbedtls_concurrent_large_response_test.v` failed identically but was MASKED on CI by the known-flaky-file list (`VTEST_FAIL_FLAKY=0` swallows it after 3 retries) — locally it failed 3/3 deterministically. **Why missed:** present-but-not-run — [[agents-md-vnew-workflow]] already says "tests are a floor; run veb/net consumers for client changes," but the sweep wasn't executed for Phase 4; also the initial CI triage pattern-matched "master is broken everywhere" (true for 8 of 9 jobs that day) and only per-job signature comparison against master's own runs caught the one PR-caused failure. **Second-order miss inside the fix itself:** the probe's close is observed server-side as a CLEAN 0-byte read (close_notify EOF), NOT a read error — the first fix attempt skipped only read-error conns, so the chunked server wrote its entire response into the dead probe conn and the real request still got refused; caught by a scratch repro with server-side logging, fixed as `n <= 0`. **Mechanical sweep for next time:** `grep -l new_ssl_listener vlib -r` ∩ files referencing `fetch(`/`get_text(`/`req.do()` → run each on native Windows; and treat any "*FAILURE* of the known flaky test file ... is ignored" line in a green run as a failure to read, not noise. Class: **a change to cross-module-visible behavior (connection counts, framing, timing) needs its consumer-test sweep run across ALL of vlib, not the changed module's suite — and the flaky-ignore list can hide a deterministic regression behind a green summary line.** Commit `5c81eb79b8`. |

**Process discoveries this same round, not code-review misses but load-bearing for
trusting any future "green" claim on this file:** `transport_h2_test.v`'s real test
bodies are gated behind `$if windows && !no_vschannel ? { return }` — without
`-d no_vschannel` on native Windows every test in the file "passes" having run zero
assertions, discovered only because a buggy-vs-fixed comparison showed IDENTICAL
results with zero variation (a strong tell a test isn't actually running). Two of
this round's six fixes (findings 4 and 6) were "verified" this way at least once
before the flag was found — see [[h2-pool-tests-need-no-vschannel-flag]]. Separately,
comparing two `&H2MuxConn` pointers via bare `==` can trigger a V compiler bug (full
`H2Transport` interface-equality codegen, fails on `ssl.SSLConn`'s opaque C fields)
that only manifests when `v test <file>` bundles ALL sibling `_test.v` files in the
same package together — a quick `-o out.c <file>` bisection build does NOT reproduce
it, so a fast manual check can give false confidence that a change compiles.
Workaround: `voidptr(a) == voidptr(b)` instead of direct struct-pointer `==`.

| ecdsa key leak: missing `.free()` on a new test's PrivateKey/PublicKey | `8084407c30` | `detect_ecdsa_key_missing_free.sh` (new Angle A bullet) | `vlib/net/quic/tls13_client_hello_test.v`'s `test_build_client_hello_structure` called `ecdsa.PrivateKey.new()!` and `priv.public_key()!` but never `.free()`'d either — confirmed via ASan LeakSanitizer on PR vlang/v#27680's `sanitize-address-clang` CI job (4232 bytes / 92 allocations leaked, top frame `CRYPTO_zalloc` ← `crypto__ecdsa__PrivateKey__static__new`). Every OTHER ecdsa test (`ecdsa_test.v`, `util_test.v`, `ecdsa_p256_ecdh_test.v`) and every other net.quic test using ecdsa keys (`tls13_handshake_test.v`, `tls13_certificate_chain_test.v`) already frees both — this was the one place a brand-new test skipped the established, universally-followed convention. Not a Codex finding — found by the user directly ("Memory leak and heap-buffer overflow should have been caught during /vreview") after CI sanitizer jobs on #27680 surfaced it; no `/vreview` pass had run a mechanical check for this bug class before. **Why local review missed it:** no existing angle asked "does this new test's C-interop resource constructor have a matching `.free()` anywhere in the file" — Angle A/G's `unsafe{}`/C-interop bullets cover pointer lifetime and self-referential structs, not a plain missing-finalizer leak on a type whose OWN module doc says `// Dont forget to call .free() after finish with your key.` Fix: `defer { priv.free() }` / `defer { pub_key.free() }` right after each constructor call — verified `public_key()` uses `EVP_PKEY_dup` (a real duplicate, not a shared pointer), so freeing both is not a double-free. **Repro:** `detect_ecdsa_key_missing_free.sh vlib/net/quic/tls13_client_hello_test.v 8084407c30` flags lines 162/163 LEAKED (exit 1); clean on the fixed tree (exit 0), verified in WSL (git-bash hangs on this script's per-line subshell loop). Class: **any NEW test that constructs a C-interop resource with a documented manual-free contract needs the SAME from-scratch scrutiny as a repeat-offender function (Phase 0 step 6) — being new, freshly-written code is not a reason for less scrutiny of a well-established resource-lifetime convention already followed everywhere else in the same module.** |
| mbedtls PEM parse: `.bytes()`-derived buffer given a manual `+1` NUL-terminator length | `60d5c3e5b4` (also present at `9dfc87c428`, `8084407c30` did not touch this) | `detect_bytes_nul_terminator_mismatch.sh` (new Angle A bullet) | `vlib/net/mbedtls/x509_standalone.v:177-178`'s `verify_certificate_chain` and its own test `x509_standalone_test.v:27-29` both did `pem := <string>.bytes()` then passed `usize(pem.len + 1)` to `mbedtls_x509_crt_parse` — mbedTLS's PEM-vs-DER sniff requires a REAL trailing NUL byte in the buffer (it checks `buf[buflen-1]=='\0'`), but `.bytes()` allocates a fresh `[]u8` of EXACTLY `.len` bytes with no such byte, so the `+1` reads one byte past the heap allocation. Confirmed via ASan on PR vlang/v#27680's `sanitize-address-clang-without-gc` CI job: `heap-buffer-overflow ... in mbedtls_x509_crt_parse`, `READ of size 1 ... 0 bytes to the right of` an allocation whose size exactly matched the source string's length. The pre-existing, correct sibling pattern in the SAME module (`ssl_connection.c.v`'s `new_sslcerts_in_memory_with_rng`, 4 call sites) passes `<string>.str` (V's own internally NUL-terminated C-string buffer) instead of `.bytes()` — this new file duplicated the mistake in BOTH its production function and its own test, never checking the sibling. Not a Codex finding — same user report as the leak above. **Why local review missed it:** Phase 0 step 7 ("find the closest existing sibling ... diff intent line-by-line") calls for exactly this comparison but wasn't run against `ssl_connection.c.v`'s existing PEM-parsing call sites when this NEW file was first written; the file's own doc comment even discusses the DER path's NUL-terminator rules at length (for `build_certificate_chain`) without cross-checking the PEM path two functions below it against the module's own established convention. Fix: use `<string>.str` directly in both files. **Repro:** `detect_bytes_nul_terminator_mismatch.sh vlib/net/mbedtls/x509_standalone.v 60d5c3e5b4` and the `_test.v` sibling both flag MISMATCH (exit 1); clean on the fixed tree (exit 0), verified against `ssl_connection.c.v` (0 false positives) in WSL. Class: **when a new file introduces a second call site for a C parsing function that already has established, correct callers elsewhere in the same module, diff the new call's buffer-construction against an EXISTING correct call for the same function — not just against the new file's OWN other call sites (which can share the same mistake, as both did here).** |

| ecdsa key leak, second instance: struct-field-threaded key discarded via `_` at a CALLER, not at the constructor | `66808d3002` (found by CI `sanitize-address-clang`/`sanitize-address-gcc`, not Codex) | `detect_ecdsa_key_missing_free.sh` (extended to recognize `generate_key(`/`from_uncompressed_bytes(`; still cannot verify THIS specific fix, confirmed false positive) + NEW `detect_struct_wrapped_resource_leak.sh` (does verify it -- see below) | `tls13_handshake_test.v`'s `drive_to_wait_encrypted_extensions` helper correctly generates and frees its OWN two local keys (`server_pub` via `defer`, `client_pub` via `defer`) but returns a THIRD key (`server_priv`) bundled inside a `FakeServerHandshake.priv_key` field. 7 of the file's test functions called this helper and discarded the returned struct as `mut h, _ := ...`, dropping the only reference to that field's raw OpenSSL `EVP_PKEY*` (`ecdsa.PrivateKey.free()` has no GC backing or self-guard -- this same file's own `test_free_is_idempotent` already documents that both a missed free and a double free are real bugs here, not theoretical). **Corrected count** (the ORIGINAL version of this row said 8 -- wrong; recounted precisely with the new detector below): 5 sibling call sites (via `drive_to_certificate`) already captured the struct as `server` and freed `server.priv_key` in their `defer` block, plus 1 more relay call inside `drive_to_certificate` itself that correctly forwards the struct onward for one of those 5 to free -- 6 already-correct, 7 broken, 13 total call sites across both helper functions, not "15" as an earlier draft of this row also said. 3 of the 7 leaking functions were themselves NEW in round 5 (added as regression tests for that round's OWN findings), so round 5's `/vreview` had a direct opportunity to catch this by diffing the 3 new test functions against the file's pre-existing siblings and never did. **Why local review missed it:** the existing `detect_ecdsa_key_missing_free.sh` (built for the FIRST ecdsa-leak miss, see the row above) only recognized `PrivateKey.new(`/`public_key(` as constructors -- it had never been taught about `generate_key(`, the constructor this file actually uses, so it could not have caught this even if run. Separately, even after teaching it `generate_key(`, that detector's core model (grep the SAME variable name for a matching `.free()` anywhere in the file) is structurally blind to this exact shape: the key crosses a struct-field rename (`server_priv` variable -> `priv_key` field) and a function return before any caller frees it, so `server_priv.free()` never appears as literal text anywhere in the file even on the FIXED tree (verified: it still reports `server_priv` as LEAKED after the fix -- a confirmed false positive, not a bug in the fix). **Fix:** for each of the 7 functions, capture the struct (`mut h, server := ...`) and add `unsafe { server.priv_key.free() }` to the existing `defer` block, matching the 6 already-correct call sites. **Corrected, not just accepted:** the first write-up of this row claimed the struct-threading shape "needs a caller-enumeration check, not a single-file grep... needs per-caller enumeration, which a single-file grep cannot do" and left it there. The user directly asked "why can't we implement this" -- and the honest answer, on reflection, is that it's a ONE-HOP indirection (struct field of the resource type -> its enclosing struct -> every function returning that struct and which position -> every call site's binding there), fully resolvable by chaining two greps, NOT genuine data-flow/alias analysis. Built `detect_struct_wrapped_resource_leak.sh` and Phase-R'd it: flags all 7 on `66808d3002` (exit 1), clean (0 flags) on the fixed tree (exit 0) -- a real, non-false-positive verification the OTHER detector still can't give. What this new detector genuinely does NOT handle: the struct being re-aliased to a different variable before the free, the resource nested two-plus levels of struct deep, or (as first shipped) a caller enumeration that must span MULTIPLE files. **The cross-file gap did NOT need to stay open either** -- see the very next row: it's now closed for real via a Graphify code-graph integration, not left as a stated limitation. Class: **(1) a resource-leak detector keyed on "this exact constructor spelling" needs updating every time a NEW constructor spelling for the same leaking type is introduced -- a clean run only proves absence of the SPELLINGS THE DETECTOR KNOWS ABOUT, not absence of the bug class; (2) before declaring a shape "needs real data-flow tracing" and stopping there, try the one-hop version first -- struct-field indirection is a single, chainable grep away, not a compiler-grade problem, and asserting otherwise without attempting it is itself a review miss worth catching.** Commit `d263f9195f`. |
| closing the cross-file gap: Graphify code-graph integration for `detect_struct_wrapped_resource_leak.sh` | (follow-up to the row above, same day, no new bug) | `detect_struct_wrapped_resource_leak.sh` extended with an opt-in `GRAPHIFY_EXE`/`GRAPHIFY_GRAPH` env-var mode | User pushed further on the row above's stated "callers in OTHER files... single-file scoped" limitation and pointed at the Graphify MCP (`S:\vProjects\graphify`, `graphify.exe extract <path> --out <dir>` builds a queryable symbol/call graph; `graphify explain <fn>` prints real cross-file `called by` data with file:line for every caller in the WHOLE codebase, no MCP restart needed since the CLI itself works standalone against any `--graph <file>`). Extracted a graph from the `http3-quic-foundation` worktree branch (the default Graphify instance was indexing `S:\repo\vlang`'s `master`, which has no `vlib/net/quic` at all -- confirmed empirically before assuming the tool was broken). `graphify explain drive_to_wait_encrypted_extensions`/`drive_to_certificate` confirmed BOTH have zero out-of-file callers (Phase 9's real `QuicConn` consumer doesn't exist yet) -- so the single-file assumption happened to be correct for THIS bug, but not provably so before checking. **Proof the gap was real, not hypothetical:** `graphify explain build_certificate_chain` (a sibling resource-constructing function, same file family, different resource type) returned SIX distinct caller files (`x509_standalone_chain_test.v`, `x509_standalone_signature_test.v`, `tls13_certificate_chain.v`, `tls13_certificate_chain_test.v`, `tls13_handshake_test.v`, `tls13_quiche_vector_test.v`) -- a single-file-scoped run against any ONE of them would see only a fraction of its true callers. Verified the new parsing logic against this REAL 6-file case (extracts all 6 paths correctly) and regression-tested the graphify-enabled code path against the already-known-clean `tls13_handshake_test.v` case (identical FREED/line-number output with and without graphify enabled). **Bonus, unplanned finding while doing this:** `crypto.ecdsa.PrivateKey` (fully-qualified, since `PrivateKey` is itself ambiguous -- ed25519/curve25519/mldsa each have their own) has exactly ONE OTHER raw-typed field anywhere in the codebase besides the one already fixed: `Tls13ClientHandshake.ecdhe_private` (`tls13_handshake.v:145`, the PRODUCTION handshake struct, not a test fixture). Checked its own construction path (`Tls13ClientHandshake.start`, `graphify explain` shows exactly 4 callers, all in `tls13_handshake_test.v`) -- all 4 capture the handshake object itself (never `_`) and free it correctly; no live bug, but this is a check my original manual review of the test-file leak never thought to run against the PRODUCTION struct's own field, and the graph made it a 30-second query instead of a full-file re-read. Also confirmed a REAL 2-level-nested-resource case exists in this exact codebase: `VerifiedCertificateChain` (`tls13_certificate_chain.v:13`, itself a one-hop wrapper around `&C.mbedtls_x509_crt`) is a field of `Tls13ClientHandshake` (`graphify explain VerifiedCertificateChain`'s "referenced by" list) -- already handled correctly BY HAND (`Tls13ClientHandshake.free()` calls `h.verified_chain.free()`), not a live bug, but proof the 2-level shape is not hypothetical either. **What remains a genuine, confirmed hard limit, not solved and not attempted further:** re-aliasing a struct to a different local variable before the free. This is NOT a "didn't get to it yet" gap like the cross-file one was -- `graphify overview`'s own symbol-kind list (module/import/fn/struct/field/const/method/enum/interface) has NO local-variable/assignment node at all, so no graph query, however cleverly composed, can ever answer "does this SPECIFIC local get reassigned before this SPECIFIC free call" -- that needs a real AST/dataflow pass over a function body, a genuinely different class of tool than a symbol/call graph. Generalizing the 2-level-nesting case to full N-level recursion was also explicitly NOT done (a deliberate scope cut: no live N-level ecdsa-key bug motivates it today; the recursion would use the same "referenced by" edges already proven to work for the 2-level VerifiedCertificateChain check, so it's buildable later if a real case needs it). Class: **before declaring ANY limitation of a mechanical check "structural" or "needs deeper analysis," ask what KIND of question it actually is -- "which files call this function" is a graph-reachability question (a code graph answers it directly, verify empirically rather than assume single-file scope is fine); "does this specific local var get reassigned" is a dataflow question (a symbol graph structurally cannot answer it, no matter how the query is phrased) -- conflating the two, as the row above initially did, undersells what's actually fixable and oversells what isn't.** No new commit (tooling-only; the underlying code fix from the row above is unchanged). |

wsl -e bash -lc 'cd /mnt/s/repo/vlang && bash .claude/skills/code-review/detect_ecdsa_key_missing_free.sh vlib/net/quic/tls13_client_hello_test.v'                   # fixed tree: exit 0
wsl -e bash -lc 'cd /mnt/s/repo/vlang && bash .claude/skills/code-review/detect_bytes_nul_terminator_mismatch.sh vlib/net/mbedtls/x509_standalone.v 60d5c3e5b4'      # exit 1
wsl -e bash -lc 'cd /mnt/s/repo/vlang && bash .claude/skills/code-review/detect_bytes_nul_terminator_mismatch.sh vlib/net/mbedtls/x509_standalone.v'                 # fixed tree: exit 0

wsl -e bash -lc 'bash /mnt/s/repo/vlang/.claude/skills/code-review/detect_ecdsa_key_missing_free.sh /mnt/s/repo/vlang-http3-foundation/vlib/net/quic/tls13_handshake_test.v'                    # fixed tree: STILL exit 1 -- confirmed false positive on `server_priv`, see the row above; do not treat this detector's output on this file as authoritative

wsl -e bash -lc 'bash /mnt/s/repo/vlang/.claude/skills/code-review/detect_struct_wrapped_resource_leak.sh /mnt/s/repo/vlang-http3-foundation/vlib/net/quic/tls13_handshake_test.v'                # fixed tree: exit 0, genuinely clean

| WebFetch truncates before reaching late RFC sections | n/a -- methodology gap, not a code bug | none (new technique, not a detector) | Verifying two Codex findings on PR vlang/v#27880 (retry.v's DCID/SCID field semantics, RFC 9000 §17.2.5.1; CRYPTO frame's 2^62-1 bound, §19.6) against the primary RFC text repeatedly failed: `WebFetch` on `rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9000.html`/`.txt` kept summarizing only the first few thousand lines and reporting "section not included in the provided excerpt" for anything past roughly §5-7, even though §17/§19 are real sections later in the SAME document -- the underlying single-shot summarization has a content budget the ~8500-line RFC exceeds. **Fix: download the plain-text RFC locally** (`curl -sL https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc<N>.txt -o rfc<N>.txt`) to `.claude/skills/code-review/rfc-texts/` (gitignored -- confirmed via `git check-ignore`), then grep/read the exact section directly with the dedicated Search tool instead of re-fetching. Also checked errata (`errata.rfc-editor.org/search/?rfc_number=<N>` via WebFetch -- the `errata_search.php?format=json` endpoint is a JS-rendered page curl cannot scrape, returns only the search-form shell) before trusting the cached text as final; confirmed none of the sections actually used (§6.2, §12.2, §14.1, §17.2.5, §19.3, §19.6) have outstanding errata. Now has RFC 9000/9001/9002/8446/7301 cached locally with a README documenting the errata-check status and refresh command -- | Unshared getter returns internal mutable state by reference | `72a6269e1e` (pre-fix) | `detect_unshared_getter_return.sh` (new Angle A bullet) | Codex round 4 on PR vlang/v#27880: `CryptoStreamReassembler.data()` (`crypto_stream.v`) returned `r.received` directly, no `.clone()` -- V's plain array assignment shares backing storage, so `mut d := r.data(); d[0] = x` corrupted the reassembler's own CRYPTO transcript, despite its own doc comment saying "this is a growing snapshot, not a destructive drain". `data()` was never touched by any of the three prior review rounds on this same file (F4, F8, the merge_or_add_pending redesign, the unsafe-removal round) -- the repeat-offender full-contract-re-derivation rule was being applied only to functions with a fresh diff each round, not to every function in a file that already qualified as a repeat offender. Built and Phase-R verified a mechanical detector (exit 1 on `72a6269e1e`, exit 0 on the `.clone()` fix), then pointed it at the rest of the module: immediately found two more, still-open instances one file away (`Tls13ClientHandshake.application_secrets()`/`handshake_secrets()` in `tls13_handshake.v`, each returning a struct with `[]u8` secret fields -- ApplicationSecrets/HandshakeSecrets), proof the detector generalizes past the one instance it was written for. Flagged those two via a separate task rather than fixing inline (different phase/branch, zero current callers). **Repro:** `bash .claude/skills/code-review/detect_unshared_getter_return.sh vlib/net/quic/crypto_stream.v 72a6269e1e` -> exit 1, flags line 67; same command with no revision arg (working tree, post-fix) -> exit 0. Also caught and fixed a digit-omission bug in the detector's OWN first draft while doing this (`[a-zA-Z_]*` excluded identifiers containing digits like `u8`/`Tls13ClientHandshake`, silently missing every V primitive type name and this exact struct) -- the same class of "detector never actually tested against a real positive" gap the gawk `\b` bug already warned about; caught here specifically BECAUSE Phase-R against the known-bad commit was run before trusting a clean scan, not skipped. **Both flagged instances since fixed and independently verified:** `crypto_stream.v`'s `data()` in commit `0601fc7483` (`http3-quic-initial-exchange`), and `tls13_handshake.v`'s `application_secrets()`/`handshake_secrets()` in commit `6eaac75892` (`http3-quic-handshake-completion`, PR #27881) -- both clone every `[]u8` field, both add a regression test proving independence (the `tls13_handshake.v` test uses `unsafe` raw-pointer writes since V's checker already blocks the naive mutation/alias paths on a `pub:` field), both re-verified here via `detect_unshared_getter_return.sh` (clean except `state()`'s confirmed-safe enum return) and a full `net.quic` test run (21/21 and 24/24 passing respectively) rather than trusting either session's self-report. |

| Stale/wrong ⏳ DEFERRED rows in a standing conformance matrix | `vlib/net/quic/coalesce.v` `split_coalesced_datagram` | `quic_conformance_matrix.md` §14.1 rows (new); no mechanical detector, matrix IS the detector | Codex round 6 on PR vlang/v#27880 (pullrequestreview-4839675790): `split_coalesced_datagram` never checked the overall datagram size, so an Initial packet in a UDP datagram under RFC 9000 §14.1's 1200-byte floor was still returned -- no compliant peer, client or server, can legitimately produce one this small (both sides pad Initial-carrying datagrams to that floor). Fixed by excluding just the undersized packet from the result (mirroring the file's existing DCID-mismatch skip-and-continue pattern), not erroring the whole call; 3 existing synthetic-datagram tests needed repadding to keep exercising their OWN original scenario instead of tripping the new check (one required inflating the leading packet's own payload rather than trailing zero-padding, since padding AFTER a malformed length-overrun chunk would have silently supplied enough bytes to make its bogus oversized Length claim look satisfiable). **The self-found part, larger than the original finding:** the matrix already had a row for this exact requirement, written `⏳ DEFERRED` before Phase 4 landed on this branch -- conflating BOTH halves of §14.1 (send-side padding, which IS implemented, and receive-side discard, which was NOT) under one send-side-sounding title, so even a from-scratch re-check of the row could plausibly have stopped at `pad_initial_payload` and missed the other half. Sweeping every other `⏳` row in the matrix for the same staleness pattern (grep `⏳`, cross-check the cited file against `git log <base>..HEAD -- <file>`) found 3 more: two reserved-bits rows (long- and short-header) that were fully resolved by `header_protection.v`'s `reserved_bits_mask` check once Phase 3 landed, and -- worse -- a Version-Negotiation-discard row whose own WRITTEN DESCRIPTION had the RFC 9000 §6.2 requirement backwards ("discard when unsupported, error when v1 present," the exact inverse of the real rule and of the correct, already-fixed, Codex-verified code from finding F1). A wrong row is more dangerous than a missing one: a future reviewer trusting it could "fix" correct code to match it. Root cause: the matrix's OWN "How to use" section already stated the rule ("re-audit `⏳` rows once their phase lands, don't assume it's someone else's problem forever") but the rule was never actually executed across five full Codex rounds on this exact code -- a written rule that isn't tied to a checkable trigger doesn't get followed.
